| `random` takes one uniform number per life: the closed-form predictive's quantile at it; otherwise the number picks a posterior point by the cumulative weights and its place within that point's share, rescaled to (0, 1) and held inside it, is the probability for that point's own quantile. On a grid the life is drawn at the grid point itself, not at a point jittered within its cell (`Posterior.draws` and `draw_models` still jitter). | proposed | Half the random numbers (a quarter on a two-parameter grid), and the draws then follow exactly the distribution `sf`, `ff` and `qf` describe, which is the mixture over the grid points; the jittered draws followed a slightly smoother one. Stratifying the uniforms over the points was the alternative; it would make draws depend on `size`. | proposed |
| The same seed gives different lives than in the last release. Listed under **Changed** in the changelog with the old and new draws; no deprecation step. | principle 19; Deprecation Policy, "Results that may change" | Principle 19 promises that a seed reproduces a draw within a version, not that the stream is the same across versions; the policy has a deliberate change to a result listed under Changed. A warning could not carry this change. | proposed |
| `compare_to(on="ff")` compares the points' own `ff` (`quantity_at(..., ("F", x))`), for both a Bayesian and a maximum-likelihood other model. | principle 1 | `1 - sf` of values near 1 compares rounding, and the other model's delta-method standard error was the difference of two such numbers. | done |
| Found, not changed: the grid's spread quantile (`lattice_quantile`) of a function and of its complement differ by about 1e-4 relative at ordinary times (its running sums of slopes lose digits in different places in the two directions). It is inside the integration error the fit reports. | proposed | Outside this change; making it symmetric would move every credible bound. Reported for the maintainer. | open |
| Found, not changed: `Posterior` renormalises its weights only when their sum is more than 1e-12 from 1. | proposed | Harmless now that nothing subtracts from the sum; left as it is so no existing number moves. | open |
### Bootstrap refits with no finite maximum (12 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Every bootstrap that refits resamples keeps a refit that returns with `maximum == "no finite maximum"` at the estimate it reached (the rule since #617 and of `Parametric.bootstrap`), and leaves out a resample the fit refuses because its likelihood has no maximum at all (no failures, only left-censored rows, fewer distinct failure times than parameters, one failure time consistent with every row: `NoFiniteMaximumError`, a `ValueError`). Applied to the parametric and regression bounds (unchanged), `DegradationAnalysis.cb` and `DestructiveDegradation.cb` (now counted), and counted in the univariate and competing-risks goodness-of-fit bootstraps. | proposed | Keeping is the faithful bootstrap: the sampling distribution of the estimate includes those run-offs, and a percentile interval needs their rank more than their value. Dropping them narrows the bounds below the likelihood-ratio ones: on a Weibull PH fit with 2 failures in one of two groups of 20, 28 of 400 refits ran off; the 95% percentile interval of the coefficient was [-15.59, 1.35] keeping them and [-1.58, 1.16] dropping them, against the likelihood-ratio [-2.10, 1.40]. A refused resample has no estimate to keep. | done |
| Refits with no finite maximum (kept or left out) are always reported, in one `BootstrapRefitWarning` carrying the counts (`no_maximum`, `no_estimate`, `unverified`, `failed`, `n_boot`), not only above 2% of the refits; unverified refits and other failures keep the 2% allowance. `Parametric.bootstrap()` adds `no_estimate`. | principle 22 | Below 2% they could still set a bound (with 99% bounds from 200 refits the bound is the 1st to 2nd refit), and the bound then depends on where a run-off search stopped (the AH weights case in `conformance/test_weights.py`). | done |
| Not changed: the recurrent Cramer-von Mises bootstrap still redraws a replicate whose refit raises (fewer than 2 simulated events) and does not count refits with no finite maximum; a jackknife refit (BCa of limited-failure and zero-inflated models) that raises is still left out uncounted. | proposed | Out of this change's scope; p-values and an acceleration constant, not bounds. | gap |

## 2026-11-11

### Adjusted survival curves: `direct_adjustment`, `weight_diagnostics` (11 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Direct adjustment is a function of a fitted regression model, `direct_adjustment(model, group, ...)`, returning a result record `AdjustedCurves` (in `surpyval/univariate/adjusted.py`, exported lazily at the top level); it is not a fitter and is out of the conformance registry's scope, as `sf_diff` and `PseudoRegressionResult` are. Nothing is fitted: the regression and the weights are the caller's. | proposed | It averages the predictions the regression models already make; a second fitter would duplicate them. | done |
| The default population is the rows the model was fitted to, weighted `n w`; `Z` (and `w`) give another. A formula model needs the DataFrame as `Z`: the group is set in the frame and the formula rebuilds its columns, so interactions with the group are right. | proposed | Setting a design column of a formula model would leave its interaction columns at the old group. | done |
| The variance has two parts: the model's estimation error (delta method through the parameters' covariance; for Cox the baseline's variance and the coefficients' part, as `CoxPH.cb`) and, with `covariates="sampled"` (the default), the spread of the units' predictions, `sum n (w/N)^2 (v - vbar)^2`; `covariates="fixed"` leaves the second out. The two are added as independent. | proposed | The curve is usually read as about the population the sample came from; a unit's score has mean zero given its covariates, so the cross term vanishes for a correct model. Model-based, not influence-function (robust), errors: 400 replicates of n = 200 give mean SE within 3% of the estimates' SD; `riskRegression::ate`'s errors on `lung` differ by 4% to 13%: the covariate term agrees exactly and the gap is its empirical (sandwich) influence function for the Cox fit against survfit's model-based variance, the same gap one unit's curve shows (reference tests). | done |
| Two curves of one model are compared by methods of the result (`sf_diff`, `rmst_diff`, the standard error of the difference itself), not by `surpyval.sf_diff`, which adds two independent groups' variances and stays the comparison of two weighted Kaplan-Meier fits. Curve bounds are Wald on the logit; differences are `difference +- z se`. | proposed | The curves share the model and the units; `surpyval.sf_diff`'s independence would be wrong for them. | done |
| Bounds are built for `ParametricRegressionModel` and unstratified `CoxPH`. Any other model with `sf(x, Z)` gives curves, and `cb`, `sf_diff`, `rmst`, `rmst_diff` refuse naming those families and the bootstrap by refitting; a stratified Cox model is refused; a Cox `rmst` refuses a `tau` past the largest observed time. No bootstrap method is built in. | proposed | The delta method needs each family's own variance pieces; a generic case bootstrap needs a generic refit, which the package does not have. | gap |
| `weight_diagnostics(w, group, Z, extreme=10, smd=0.1)` returns `{"groups", "balance", "flags"}`, two DataFrames and a list of sentences. The standardised mean difference divides by the unweighted pooled SD, `sqrt((s_a^2 + s_b^2) / 2)`, before and after weighting; more than two groups give every pair; an effective sample size below half the group is flagged. | proposed | One scale for before and after (Austin and Stuart 2015; R `cobalt`'s default); the thresholds are the usual rules and are arguments. | done |
### Semi-parametric recurrent-event regression: `AndersenGill`, `PrenticeWilliamsPeterson`, `WeiLinWeissfeld` (11 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Three fitters in `recurrent/regression/recurrent_cox.py`, exported from `surpyval.recurrent` and the top level, each a layout of an item's rows (risk interval, stratum, cluster) handed to `CoxPH.fit` with `tl` and `strata`, and `robust_covariance(cluster)` of that fit. There is no second partial likelihood and no second sandwich. | proposed | `CoxPH` already has delayed entry, strata, tie handling, aliasing, the check of the maximum and the cluster sandwich; R's `coxph` treats these models the same way. All four fits match R on the bladder layouts. | done |
| The full names, not `PWP` / `WLW`; Prentice-Williams-Peterson is one fitter with `time_scale="total"` (default) or `"gap"`. | proposed | One name per thing, readable without knowing the acronyms; the two PWP forms are one model on two clocks. The alternative is two fitters, or the acronyms. | proposed |
| One model class, `RecurrentCoxModel`, that holds the Cox fit as `cox` (composition) rather than subclassing the Cox model. Its own surface is small: `params`, `covariance()` (robust), `naive_covariance()`, `standard_errors()`, `summary()`, `cif`, `sf`, `plot`, `diagnose`, the comparison values, `to_dict` / `from_dict`. | proposed | A Cox model's `sf`, `random`, `cb` and concordance mean something else, or nothing, for repeat events on the layout's rows; inheriting them would offer wrong answers under familiar names. `model.cox` keeps them reachable for a user who knows what the rows are. | done |
| `covariance()` and `standard_errors()` are the robust ones (items as clusters); the model-based one is `naive_covariance()` (R's word, `naive.var`), and `summary()` shows `se(coef)` (robust, with the interval, `z` and `p` from it) and `naive se(coef)`. | proposed | The default must be the valid one (principle 15): the model-based errors are wrong whenever an item's events are correlated, and for the marginal model always. The alternative is `covariance()` model-based with `robust_covariance()` beside it, as on `CoxPH`. | proposed |
| Prediction is `cif(x, Z, stratum=None)`, the recurrent family's name, with the Cox models' `stratum` for the event number. `sf` exists for the gap-time and marginal models only and refuses for the other two. | proposed | For Andersen-Gill the scaled cumulative baseline is the expected number of events (`cif`'s meaning). `exp(-cif)` is a probability of something observable only on a clock that starts at 0 for that event. | done |
| Data: the event log `fit(x, Z, i, c, n)` with `c` 0 or 1 and `n` 1; `Z` on a row is in force over the interval ending at that row, so it may change within an item (refused for the marginal model). Start-stop rows through `fit_tvc(i, xl, xr, c, Z)` (the proportional-intensity models' names), for the Andersen-Gill and Prentice-Williams-Peterson fitters. Two events of an item at one time, a missing value, and `c` of -1 or 2 are refused. An end-of-observation row at the time of the item's last event is dropped silently (it adds no time at risk). | proposed | The proportional-intensity `fit` needs covariates constant within an item; here a row-level covariate has an exact meaning, so refusing it would only force the user into `fit_tvc`. | done |
| `max_events`: for Prentice-Williams-Peterson, what follows an item's `max_events`-th event is left out; for Wei-Lin-Weissfeld it is the number of strata (default: the most events any item had). Later event numbers are not pooled into a last stratum. | proposed | Truncation is what the reference data sets do (`bladder2` keeps four recurrences); pooling is a modelling choice the user can make by renumbering. | proposed |
| In the gap-time layout a row's interval is measured from the item's previous event, or from the start of its first row before any event. | proposed | Reduces to `Surv(stop - start, event)` when each row is one gap, and stays a valid risk interval when a gap is split into several rows. | done |
| Gaps, each documented: no robust bound on the curve (`cif_cb` refuses, naming `summary()` and `model.cox.cb`), no `random` or simulation (excluded from the refit registry with the reason; recovery is tested on simulated processes), no weights, no penalised fit, no formula in the DataFrame forms. | proposed | The robust variance of the baseline (and so of `cif`) needs the items' influence on the baseline, a piece of its own; a step baseline with no shape beyond the data does not define a process to simulate for the conditional and marginal models. | gap |
| One pull request for the four models. | proposed | They share the one model class and the one layout function; splitting would leave the class half-used. | done |
### Fitting regressions on continuous covariate paths (11 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| A fit on `CovariatePath`s is the model's existing `fit_tvc` on the paths cut into steps, repeated with the steps halved; there is no second likelihood. It is reached the way step schedules are: item records with `time_varying_covariates`, through `fit(data)`. | proposed | The step fits are tested and cover every family (PH, PO, AH, Cox, AFT by cumulative exposure); an exact path likelihood is only worth building if stepping proves too slow or too coarse in use. | done (exact likelihood a gap) |
| Steps hold the path's value at their midpoint; each stretch between the path's breakpoints is cut into equal steps; a stretch on which the path takes one value is one step, and neighbouring equal steps are merged. Public as `CovariatePath.to_steps(t_max, steps=8, start=None)`. | proposed | The midpoint rule's error in the cumulative hazard falls with the square of the step (checked against the closed form for a linear ramp), and a flat or step-shaped path then gives exactly the fits there already were. A public method lets a user choose and inspect the steps. | done |
| In the fit, the last millionth of the step a failure falls in holds the path's value at the failure. | proposed | A failure's hazard is that of the covariate at the failure; taking the middle of its step instead leaves an error that falls only with the step's width. Without it a ramp fit needed 2,048 steps a stretch to meet the tolerance; with it, 32 to 64. Steps a user builds with `to_steps` do not have this (the schedule is theirs). | done |
| The stopping rule is on the estimates, not on the hazard: halve the steps until no estimate moves by more than 1% of its standard error (a missing standard error: 1% of `max(abs(estimate), 1)`), for at most eight halvings from 8 steps and two million rows; otherwise the last fit with one `RuntimeWarning` giving the move and the steps. The tolerance is a constant, not a fit argument. | proposed | The error of a step approximation depends on the coefficients, which are not known before the fit, so a tolerance on the hazard cannot be met in advance; what the user cares about is whether the answer still depends on the steps. A new argument on every fitter's `fit` for one tolerance is not worth its surface; a user who wants other steps passes `to_steps` schedules. | proposed |
| The fit's report is the model attribute `path_steps` (a dict: `steps`, `rows`, `change`, `tolerance`, `converged`), set only by a fit on paths. It is not saved by `to_dict`. | proposed | It describes how the fit was computed, not the model; a restored model predicts the same without it. | done |
| On a baseline defined below 0 an item with a path and no `left_truncation` is refused, naming `left_truncation`. | principle 2 | Such an item is followed since -inf, and a path is cut into steps over a finite stretch only. | done |
### Joint models: what the hazard follows (`link`) (11 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| The link is an argument of the fitter factory, `JointPH(dist, path_model, varying, link="value")`, not of `fit`: `"value"`, `"slope"`, `"cumulative"`, or a list of them. The fitted model holds it as `link`, a tuple in that fixed order however it was given. | proposed | It is part of the model's structure, like `path_model` and `varying`; one fitter then fits, refits and is registered as one model. | done |
| More than one link at once is allowed, each with its own effect, rather than one link a model. | proposed | "Value and slope" is the usual form of the slope model (a rising marker at a given level), and the likelihood needs only the sum of the links' terms; three single-link models could not express it. | done |
| The effects are named `marker_effect` (the value's, unchanged), `slope_effect` and `cumulative_effect`, last in `params` in that order. | principle 21 | The existing name stays; the new ones follow its pattern, so a model with the default link has the parameters it had. | done |
| `marker_reference` belongs to the value link only and is refused without it; with the slope or the area the baseline (`alpha`, `beta`) is at a slope and an area of 0, and the model's `marker_reference` is `None`. The area is of the marker itself from age 0, with no reference subtracted. | proposed | For the value a reference moves `alpha` only. Subtracting a level from the area changes the model (it multiplies the hazard by `exp(-effect * level * t)`), so it cannot be a reporting choice; the exposure above a level is fitted by subtracting the level from the readings. This is also the form R's `JM` fits, so the two can be compared. | done |
| The slope is the path model's own derivative: `PathModel.slope` and `PathModel.slope_jacobian` are new public methods, analytic for the nine built-in paths and `LinkedPathModel`, central differences in the base class. The area is by the cumulative hazard's quadrature rule (15-point Gauss-Legendre after `s = t w^4`), with no method on the path model. | proposed | Central differences at the quadrature's smallest ages lose most of their digits (the age step is relative), so the built-in paths need the exact derivative; a path of one's own keeps working without one. The area needs no new method: the rule is exact for polynomial paths to the sixth degree and accurate to 1e-8 for a logarithmic one. | done |
| A Lloyd-Lipow path with the cumulative link is refused when the fitter is built: its area from age 0 is infinite. Paths whose slope is unbounded at age 0 (logarithmic, Lloyd-Lipow, a power below 1) are not refused for the slope link. | proposed | The first has no model to fit. The second can be a proper model for some signs of the effect, and where it is not the fit reports that it could not start or did not reach a maximum. | done (the slope case a gap) |
| No accelerated failure time survival part. | proposed | With a marker that changes over time an AFT part is not the proportional hazards one rescaled: the cumulative hazard is the baseline's at an accelerated age, `H0(int_0^t exp(effect * m(s)) ds)`, so the hazard, the interval terms, the per-item mode search and its curvature are all different functions. The present likelihood is written around the Weibull proportional hazards form throughout (its quadrature substitution included), so AFT would be a second likelihood beside it, not an option of this one. | gap |
| Several markers and a combined health index are not built. | maintainer | Parked. | gap |
### Fine-Gray: the robust (sandwich) variance is the default (11 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| `FineGray`'s `covariance()`, `standard_errors()`, `p_values`, `summary()` and printout use Fine and Gray's (1999) sandwich `A^-1 [sum n (eta + psi)(eta + psi)'] A^-1`, `cmprsk::crr`'s `var`; before, the inverse information `A^-1` (`crr`'s `invinf`). `CompetingRisksProportionalHazards` with `model="Fine-Gray"` takes each cause's block from its `FineGray` fit, so it changes with it. | maintainer (2026-11-11) | The model-based variance takes the estimated censoring weights as known; the sandwich is the method's own variance and the reference software's (principles 15, 16). | done |
| A finding for that decision, as measured: the robust standard errors are mostly the smaller, not always. Over eight stored `crr` fits they run from 25% smaller to 17% larger than the model-based ones (smaller for 17 of 22 coefficients; first coefficient of the `competing` reference: 0.3315 model-based, 0.3082 robust). In the coverage study fixed before it was run (`test_fine_gray_coefficient_coverage`: n = 200, 47% censored, 1000 replicates) the 95% Wald intervals covered 94.3% and 93.2% with the robust variance and 94.8% and 94.3% with the model-based one; both are inside the suite's tolerance. The default is set in one place, `fine_gray.ROBUST_BY_DEFAULT`, which `covariance`, `standard_errors` and `summary` read; `p_values` and the dict's `"se"` follow the robust covariance and would need the same switch. | proposed | So the maintainer can weigh the default against the numbers, and reverse it cheaply. | open |
| The choice is made where the variance is read, by `robust` (default `True`) on `covariance`, `standard_errors` and `summary`, not by an argument of `fit`. No `robust_covariance()` beside it (it would be a second name for `covariance()`), and no `robust` on `CompetingRisksProportionalHazards` (its Cox path has no robust form there; the model-based Fine-Gray covariance of a cause is `models[cause].covariance(robust=False)`). | proposed | `robust` is the package's one name for this choice (`summary(robust=)`, `cb(robust=)` on the regressions), and the fit is the same either way (the 2026-10-29 row "not a `robust=` argument to `fit`"). Here the default is the robust one, so the option reads `robust=False`. | proposed |
| `p_values` stays an attribute, on the default (robust) standard errors; the model-based p-values are the `p` column of `summary(robust=False)`. | proposed | One coefficient shows one uncertainty per call ("Robust all or none"); a `model_based_p_values()` would be a name with no relative. | proposed |
| A dict stores the robust covariance under `"covariance"` (the key of `covariance()`), with `"se"` and `"p_values"` from it, and the inverse information under `"model_covariance"`. No schema change: an older reader reads `"covariance"` as the model's covariance, which it now is. | principle 20, the 2026-11-05 rule "each matrix under its own name" | Both survive a round trip. | done |
| A dict saved before this (no `"model_covariance"`, not weighted) holds the model-based covariance under `"covariance"`. It loads as that: `covariance(robust=False)` and `summary(robust=False)` as before, while `covariance()`, `standard_errors()` and `summary()` refuse, saying to ask for `robust=False` or refit, and `p_values` is `nan`. A `CompetingRisksProportionalHazards` Fine-Gray model made of such dicts refuses `covariance()` the same way. | proposed | The robust covariance needs the data; "an attribute that cannot be known is left unset rather than filled with the wrong matrix" (2026-11-03). The alternative, returning the old numbers from `covariance()`, would give one call two meanings by the age of the file. | proposed |
| Counts `n` are units in the sandwich: a row with `n = 3` is three units, as everywhere (checked against the rows repeated, and against `crr` on repeated rows). | principle 5 | — | done |
| A weighted fit (`w`) gets Fine and Gray's sandwich too, the censoring term `psi` included, with sampling weights: every sum in `eta` and `psi` (risk sets, baseline increments, the censoring Kaplan-Meier and its at-risk counts) taken with `n w`, and the meat `sum n w^2 (eta + psi)(eta + psi)'`. So `w = 1` gives exactly the unweighted covariance (checked to 1e-12), scaling every weight changes nothing, and the sums are checked against the definitions with weights. The part without `psi` still matches R's `coxph(..., weights = fgwt * w, cluster = id)` on `finegray`'s data (1e-6). Whole-number weights give the counted fit's estimates exactly but not its standard errors: a weight is one unit standing for `w` (sampling weight), a count is `n` units (as in `docs/Weighted Data.rst` and every other weighted fit). A weighted fit has no model-based covariance (`robust=False` refuses). | proposed | Unit weights must reproduce the unweighted fit; leaving `psi` out made them differ under censoring. Weighted `psi` has no R reference (`crr` takes no weights). | done |
| Where a coefficient runs off, the robust covariance is `nan` wherever a risk set's sum underflows (the other coefficients' entries can be `nan` too, where the model-based ones were finite). | proposed | The fit has already warned "No finite maximum"; the influence terms are not defined there. | done |
| `robust=` is in the conformance options sweep as the property `covariance_robust`: on every registered model whose `covariance()` takes it (`ROBUST_COVARIANCE`, which a test keeps complete), both values give a symmetric positive semi-definite covariance that `standard_errors()` and `summary()` follow, with the same default in all three; and a naming convention, `robust=` defaults to `False` everywhere except `FineGrayModel` (`True`). | proposed | The sweep's other parts are of bounds and fit options; `robust=` is a method option, so it gets a property and a convention of its own. | done |
### Parametric competing-risks regression: `ParametricCompetingRisksRegression` (11 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| One class, `ParametricCompetingRisksRegression`, in `univariate/competing_risks/regression`, exported from `surpyval.univariate.competing_risks` and the top level. Fitter and fitted model in one, like `ParametricCompetingRisks` (class-method fits, the per-cause models in `models[cause]`). Each cause is fitted by the existing regression fitter's own `fit` with the other causes' failures right-censored; there is no second likelihood. | proposed | The likelihood of cause-specific hazard models factorises over the causes, so reuse is exact, and no independence of the latent times is assumed. | done |
| The argument naming the per-cause fitter is `model` (`model=WeibullPH`, or `{cause: fitter}`), not `dist`. | proposed | `ParametricCompetingRisks` takes `dist` because it takes distributions; these are regression models, and a cause may take a PH, an AFT or a PO model. `CompetingRisksProportionalHazards` already uses `model` for the kind of per-cause model. | proposed |
| A fitter must be a parametric regression fitter (the fitters sharing `DataFrameRegressionMixin`); anything else (a distribution, `CoxPH`) is refused, naming `CompetingRisksProportionalHazards` for Cox. | principle 2 | A Cox model has no hazard past the data, which is the model's purpose. | done |
| `Z` comes after the times in every function and is paired with them as the regressions pair it (one row at every time, a row per time, or several rows at one time); `event` follows `Z`. `probability_of_cause(Z, event)` and `mean(Z)` give one value per row. | principle 7, 21 | The regressions' rule; `CompetingRisksProportionalHazards.cif(x, Z, event)`'s order. | done |
| Right censoring, counts and delayed entry only. Left- and interval-censored rows are refused (their likelihood term holds every cause at once, as in `ParametricCompetingRisks`' joint fit, which has no covariates yet); weights are refused. | proposed (the every-bound rule) | A joint fit with covariates is a new likelihood, and a weighted one needs the sandwich across causes; each is its own piece of work. | gap |
| Bounds: Wald on `cif` (logit scale, central differences over every cause's parameters) and on the all-cause `sf`/`ff`/`Hf` (log cumulative hazard, the causes' variances added), the scales and rules of `ParametricCompetingRisks.cb`; a cause's net bounds are its own model's `cb` (every method it has). Likelihood-ratio and bootstrap bounds on the incidence and the all-cause functions refuse, naming Wald. | principle 18, the every-bound rule | One rule for the two parametric competing-risks models. | done (gap: LR and bootstrap on `cif`) |
| `random(size, Z, random_state)` returns `(draws, Z)`: `draws` the structured `x`/`e` array of `ParametricCompetingRisks.random`, `Z` the row of each draw as the regressions' `random` returns it. | proposed | Both relatives' conventions at once. | proposed |
| `summary()` stacks the causes' regression tables under a first index level `cause`. | proposed | Each cause's table is its model's own, so nothing is restated. | proposed |
| Information criteria are the whole model's: summed log-likelihood, parameters over every cause, BIC's `n` the failures of any cause. | principle 21 (`ParametricCompetingRisks`) | The same rule as the model without covariates. | done |
| Not in this piece, each a documented refusal or absence: `diagnose()` on the whole model (use `models[cause].diagnose()`) and `plot`. A parametric model of the subdistribution (a parametric Fine-Gray) is a different model and is not planned here. | proposed | Kept out so that this piece stays reviewable. | open |
| The cause-by-cause assembly (the incidence integral on the cause's probability scale, the Aalen-Johansen step from hazard increments, the earliest-latent-time draw, the block-diagonal covariance, the summed information-criterion terms, the logit and log-cumulative-hazard Wald bounds) lives once, in `univariate/competing_risks/_assembly.py`, used by `ParametricCompetingRisks`, `ParametricCompetingRisksRegression` and `CompetingRisksProportionalHazards`. `ParametricCompetingRisks` stays its own class rather than the regression with an empty `Z`. | proposed | One copy of each computation; the model without covariates also has cure and zero-inflated causes, offsets and a joint fit for interval data, which the regression does not, so folding it in would change what it accepts. | done |
| Not in this piece, each a documented refusal or absence: `diagnose()` on the whole model (use `models[cause].diagnose()`), `plot`, and one shared helper for the cause-by-cause assembly with `ParametricCompetingRisks`. A parametric model of the subdistribution (a parametric Fine-Gray) is a different model and is not planned here. | proposed | Kept to a second pull request so that this one stays reviewable. | open |
### Warnings collected in one thread only (12 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| The last six places that used `catch_warnings` use thread-local blocks from `surpyval/utils/warnings.py`: `collecting_warnings(category=Warning)` for `catch_warnings(record=True)` with `simplefilter("always", category)` (`fit_best`, the renewal fit's no-maximum check, the Cox frailty fit's Cox fit, the residual-life integral, `diagnose()`'s Cramer-von Mises test), and `raising_warnings(category)` for `simplefilter("error", category)` (`formula_model_matrix`'s unknown-level check for a formula naming its levels). No module outside `surpyval/utils/warnings.py` uses `catch_warnings`; that one use is the Python 3.14 context-aware path, where the standard form is local to the thread. | proposed | While a collecting block ran, every warning raised in any other thread was shown "always" and went into that block's list, so the other thread lost it (a test saw 29,356 of another thread's warnings taken during one `fit_best`). Each site is converted one for one, so a single thread sees the same warnings, counts and messages; the refactor snapshot is bit-exact before and after. | done |
| A collecting block is the thread-local filter of the 11 Nov change with the action "always", plus a wrapper put once, for good, in front of `warnings._showwarnmsg` (the hook the interpreter hands every shown warning to, looked up on the module each time): a warning shown in a thread with a collecting block open goes to that thread's innermost block, and every other goes on to the hook that was there, so `catch_warnings(record=True)` and pytest's recording work as before. `raising_warnings` is the same filter with the action "error". | proposed | `catch_warnings(record=True)` swaps `showwarning` and `_showwarnmsg_impl` for the whole process; `_showwarnmsg` is the one hook it leaves alone. The wrapper runs after the filters are read, so the C-only rule for reading the list still holds. One difference, only in a thread that opens the standard `catch_warnings(record=True)` inside one of these blocks: the library's block, not the standard one, collects. No library code does that. | done |
| Thread-local filters of different actions keep "the newest filter wins" in each thread: a block reuses a filter of the leading run only if every filter ahead of it is off in this thread or has the same action; otherwise it adds one at the front. So an ignoring block inside a collecting one still ignores, and a collecting block inside an ignoring one collects, as with `catch_warnings`. The list grows only with how deeply blocks of different actions have been nested, never with use. | proposed | A fixed order (all "ignore" filters ahead of all "always" ones) would have stopped a collecting block opened inside an ignoring one from collecting, which changes what the renewal fit and `diagnose()` read. | done |
### A restored single-process repairable model keeps its bounds (11 Nov 2026)
### A restored single-process repairable model keeps its bounds (12 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| `ParametricRecurrenceModel.to_dict` and `ProportionalIntensityModel.to_dict` write the fit's parameter covariance (`"covariance"`, `null` for a model with no likelihood and for a penalised fit), and the restored model computes `covariance()`, `standard_errors()`, `summary()`, `param_cb`, `cif_cb`, `iif_cb`, `mtbf_cb` and `cb_tvc` from it. This closes the open row of "Several failure modes of a repairable system" below: the cause-specific models gave these after restoring and the single-process models they are made of refused. | principle 20, "every bound" | Every bound a model has before saving it has after restoring, where the dictionary can hold what the bound needs: a Wald bound needs the estimates and their covariance, nothing else. The other direction (both refuse) would have removed bounds the cause-specific models already give. | proposed |
| The key is optional on reading and the schema is unchanged: a dictionary saved earlier loads as before, and its bounds refuse with a message saying the dictionary holds no covariance and to refit. | proposed | The same rule as `CauseSpecificNHPP`'s `"covariances"`. | done |
| What needs the data still refuses on a restored model (the likelihood and criteria, diagnostics, `plot`, Crow's exact bounds); the likelihood's refusal now says which calls the saved covariance does serve. | principle 2, 22 | A refusal that says what is available as well as what is not. | done |
| `CauseSpecificNHPP` and `CauseSpecificProportionalIntensity` are unchanged: they keep reading their own `"covariances"`, so each cause's covariance is now in the dictionary twice (once in the cause's own model). | proposed | Dropping `"covariances"` would change those dictionaries for no gain to a reader; the duplication costs a few numbers per cause. The alternative is to have the containers read their models' saved covariance and stop writing their own. | proposed |
| `RenewalModel` (generalized renewal, G1 renewal, ARA, ARI) saves its `covariance()` too, the held-edge one where the repair parameter is on the edge of its range (its row and column `nan`). The restored model gives the same `covariance()`, `standard_errors()` and Wald `param_cb`, and the same `summary()` with the repair parameter inside its range. On the edge, the repair parameter's interval and so `summary()` refuse, as do `method="lr"` and the repair test: they are profile-likelihood refits, which need the data. | proposed | The same rule as the Poisson-process models: what needs only the estimates and the covariance survives saving; what needs the data refuses and says so. Saving the profile interval itself was the alternative; it depends on `alpha_ci` and `bound`, so it would serve only the one level saved. | done |
### Mixture-cure regression: `MixtureCure` (11 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| `MixtureCure(latency)` is a distributional regression of the *defective* distribution: the latency distribution's parameters plus `lfp_p`, the probability of ever failing, with a probability link. `MixtureCureFitter` and `MixtureCureModel` subclass `DistributionalFitter` and `DistributionalRegressionModel` (in `regression/distributional/mixture_cure.py`); the family's fit, checks, bounds, penalised fit, weights and saved form are reused, through four small hooks (`_check_distribution`, `_model_class`, `_say_verdict`, `_fitter_from_dict`). | proposed | The mixture likelihood is the regressions' own likelihood of `sf = 1 - p + p S_u`; a second fitting pipeline would duplicate the family's. With no covariates it is `latency.fit(lfp=True)` to the last digit of the likelihood. | done |
| The latency is a distribution with covariates on its parameters (the scale, location or rate by default: the AFT form), not "any regression fitter" (`WeibullPH`, ...). Latencies: Weibull, LogNormal, LogLogistic, Gamma, Exponential. | proposed | It is `flexsurvcure`'s model, and the distributional family's. A Weibull latency's scale coefficient `b` is a log hazard ratio `beta b`, so the Weibull PH latency is the same fit read differently; a PH latency on another baseline is not built. | gap |
| The incidence parameter is named `lfp_p`, as the univariate limited-failure fits name the proportion that ever fails; coefficients are `<parameter>_<column>` (`lfp_p_load`, `alpha_load`), the family's naming, not "incidence: load" / "latency: load". Every coefficient has SurPyval's sign: positive is a higher chance of failing, or an earlier failure. | proposed | One name per thing across the package; with covariates allowed on several latency parameters "latency: load" would not say which. | done |
| `incidence=` takes the link's name: `"logit"` (default), `"probit"`, `"cloglog"`; the three are added to the distributional links for a parameter in (0, 1). | proposed | The package names links (`"log"`, `"identity"`), not regressions. | done |
| The model's functions are the population's (`sf` levels off at the cure probability; `qf` is `inf` at and beyond `lfp_p(z)`; `mean` is `inf`; `random` draws a cured unit as `inf`), as the univariate limited-failure model's are; `susceptible=True` gives the latency's. | proposed | The univariate precedent, and the identities every model keeps (`qf(ff(x)) = x`, `sf + ff = 1`). | done |
| The likelihood-ratio bound on a quantile is `inf` above where the likelihood region allows `lfp_p(z) <= p` (decided by the profile lower bound on `lfp_p` at the row, at the same level; that side is not searched). Where `p` is above the fitted `lfp_p(z)` the quantile and its bounds stay `nan` (lower) / `inf` (upper), with the existing "could not be found" warning for the lower side. | proposed | The quantile is infinite at such parameters, so the supremum over the region is too; the search over the rest of the region gave finite upper bounds that shrank as the level grew (95%: 22.9, 99%: 20.8 on the conformance fixture). | done |
| No fit-time warning for short follow-up: `follow_up()` returns Maller and Zhou's check (`(1 - N/n)^n`, `N` the failures in `(2 t_f - t_max, t_f]`), for exact and right-censored data. Data with no cured fraction warn "No finite maximum" naming `lfp_p`, with the advice to fit the latency alone. | proposed | The check has no agreed threshold and does not apply to interval-censored or truncated data; a warning on every fit it cannot judge would be noise (principle 22), while the no-maximum case is detected by the fit's own verdict. | done |
| Conformance: the five prebuilt fitters are registered on a fixture with a plateau (`cure_data`), their `model_class` declared as `DistributionalRegressionModel` (a `MixtureCureModel` is one) so that every table keyed by the class applies. | proposed | One battery for the family. | done |
| Not built here: covariates on a dead-on-arrival fraction (zero-inflation), a Cox latency, a classifier for the incidence, covariate paths. | proposed | Each is a separate piece; the classifier route is parked. | gap |

### Zero-inflated regression: `ZeroInflated` (12 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| `ZeroInflated(latency)` is `latency.fit(zi=True)` with covariates and the mirror image of `MixtureCure`: a distributional regression of the zero-inflated distribution (the latency's parameters plus `f0`, the probability of failing at time 0, with a probability link), in `regression/distributional/zero_inflated.py`. `ZeroInflatedFitter` and `ZeroInflatedModel` subclass the family's fitter and model through the same hooks as `MixtureCure`, with the same API: `incidence=` (logit, probit, cloglog), `covariates=`/`formulas=` per part (default: every column on `f0` and on the latency's life parameter), `susceptible=True` for the latency (here, "a unit that worked"), prebuilt `WeibullZeroInflated`, `LogNormalZeroInflated`, `LogLogisticZeroInflated`, `GammaZeroInflated`, `ExponentialZeroInflated`. The share is named `f0`, the univariate fits' name; a positive coefficient on it is a higher chance of being dead on arrival. | maintainer (requested the model, both routes) | One family, one API; the univariate name for the same parameter. | done |
| Two routes to the one maximum. When every row says whether its unit was dead on arrival (a failure at 0 was; a failure or censoring after 0, or a right-censored time of 0, was not; a late entry `tl > 0` could not have been and drops out of the `f0` part), the likelihood is `prod f0 * prod (1 - f0)` times the latency's likelihood of the units that worked, with no parameter in common: stage 1 is a binary regression (dead on arrival, yes or no) with the chosen link, stage 2 is `Distributional(latency)` on the units that worked. Their estimates are the joint maximum, and the family's joint search, started there, confirms it (it does not move on the test data: 3e-16 relative) and supplies the information, which is then block diagonal. Otherwise (left censoring, an interval starting at 0, right truncation without late entry) the parts are coupled and searched jointly from the univariate zero-inflated fit. `method="auto"` (default), `"two-stage"` (refuses coupled data), `"joint"`; `model.fit_route` records the route. A fit with `fixed` or `init` is joint. | maintainer | Two stages are the exact answer when the data separate, and each stage is a well-behaved problem of its own (neither part can drag the other into a poor region); the joint search is needed only where rows couple the parts. Starting the family's search at the stages' estimates, rather than assembling a model by hand, keeps one verdict, one covariance and one saved form for both routes. | done |
| An interval that starts at 0 includes the jump at 0 ("failed by `xr`, perhaps on arrival") and is read as left censoring at `xr`; left censoring at 0, or an interval ending at 0, is a failure at 0; a left truncation at or before 0 is no truncation (and a failure at 0 with `tl = 0` is not refused). The univariate fits (`fit(zi=True)`, and `fit_bayes`) follow the same rule: left censoring at `t` and an interval `[0, t]` each contribute `f0 + (p - f0) F(t)`, an interval `(a, b]` with `a > 0` leaves the mass out, a left truncation at or below 0 is no truncation, and a late entry `tl > 0` divides by `1 - f0 - (p - f0) F(tl)`. Until 12 Nov 2026 they read an interval from 0 as `(0, xr]`, without the jump, and a `tl` of 0 as excluding it. | maintainer (approved 12 Nov 2026) | An inspection that finds a unit failed cannot say whether it ever worked; the maintainer asked for these rows to couple the parts. It is the published likelihood of zero-inflated left-censored data (Grouwels 2015, PhD thesis, ch. 2 section 2.1, p. 11; Braekers and Grouwels 2016, *Comm. Stat. Theory Methods* 45(7); Moulton and Halsey 1995, *Biometrics* 51(4)). A truncation at 0 cannot remove units that exist from 0. The no-covariate fit equals `Weibull.fit(zi=True)` on the same rows, unrewritten; an intentional change of numbers in the univariate fits, announced in the changelog. | done |
| `sf`, `ff`, `Hf`, `qf`, `mean` are the population's, right-continuous at 0 (`ff(0) = f0`, `sf(0) = 1 - f0`, `qf(u) = 0` for `u <= f0`, `mean = (1 - f0) E[T_u]`, `random` draws a unit dead on arrival as 0); `df` and `hf` are those of the failures after 0 (the jump has no density). | proposed | The univariate zero-inflated model's values, and the identities `sf + ff = 1`, `ff(qf(u)) = u` above `f0`. | done |
| Refusals: separable data with no failure at 0 ("`f0` is estimated as 0: fit the latency alone"), and separable data where every unit that could have been dead on arrival was. Where the joint route's `f0` runs to 0 the fit warns "No finite maximum" naming `f0`, with the same advice. | proposed | Principles 2 and 22: say what happened and what to fit instead. | done |
| Conformance: the five prebuilt fitters on a fixture with failures at 0 in both groups (`zi_reg_data`), declared as `DistributionalRegressionModel`. Calibration: Wald coverage of every parameter and of `sf(60 | z)`, 400 replicates. | proposed | The family's battery. | done |
| Not built: zero inflation and a cure fraction in one model, a Cox latency, covariate paths. | proposed | Separate pieces. | gap |

### Failure modes with covariates: `CauseSpecificProportionalIntensity` (11 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| One class, `CauseSpecificProportionalIntensity`, in `recurrent/competing_risks/regression`, exported from `surpyval.recurrent` and the top level beside its relatives. Like `CauseSpecificNHPP` it is fitter and fitted model in one (class-method fits, the per-type models in `models[event]`). Each type is fitted by the existing single-type fitters (`ProportionalIntensityHPP` for `baseline=HPP`, else `ProportionalIntensityNHPP`) to its own events over every item's whole window, and is an ordinary `ProportionalIntensityModel`; there is no second likelihood. `CauseSpecificNHPP`'s per-cause rows moved, unchanged, into `cause_rows`, which both models use. | proposed | The likelihood of a marked Poisson process factorises over the types, so reuse is exact; one way to build a type's rows. | done |
| Arguments: `fit` is the single-type `fit(x, Z, i, c, n, ..., baseline, init)` with `e` where `CauseSpecificNHPP.fit` has it (after `n`); the time-varying fits are the single-type ones with `e` after `n`; the DataFrame forms take `e_col` after `Z_cols`. The word is `baseline` (the regressions'), not `dist` (`CauseSpecificNHPP`'s). Predictions take the type after the covariates, `cif(x, Z, event)`, the order of `CompetingRisksProportionalHazards.cif` and of the conformance suite's call for a model with covariates and causes; `total_` in front sums over the types, and the `_tvc` forms keep `xl`, `given`, `on` after `event`. | principle 21 | No third vocabulary: every name and position comes from one of the two pieces or from the life-data competing-risks regression. | done |
| The default `baseline` is `Duane`, the single-type regression's default, not `CrowAMSAA`, `CauseSpecificNHPP`'s default `dist`. | proposed | A type's fit is then exactly `ProportionalIntensityNHPP.fit` with its defaults. The two defaults describe the same power-law process in two parameterisations, so fits agree; the names of the baseline parameters differ. The alternative is `CrowAMSAA`, matching the model without covariates. | proposed |
| Time-varying rows: a row that ends in another type's failure is, for this type, a row that ends in none (`c = 1`). A count of failures within a row (`c = 2`) is refused, in `fit` and in `fit_tvc`, naming the single-type fit to use for each type's own counts; right truncation is refused as in `CauseSpecificNHPP`. | proposed | A count carries no type per failure, and an item's rows cannot overlap to hold one count per type. | gap |
| A failure row with no type (`e` missing on a `c = 0` row) warns, with the count, and counts towards no type, in `fit_tvc` as in `fit` (whose warning is the data handler's). | principle 21, 22 | The same data problem has the same outcome through both entry points. | done |
| Totals at a covariate row and along a schedule: the types' delta-method variances add and the bounds are formed on the log scale, the rule recorded for `CauseSpecificNHPP`'s totals. A fitted type's own bounds are delegated to its model; a restored model computes them from its saved covariance with the same construction, so a one-type model's total bounds equal that type's (tested for every baseline, bound side and both `on=`). Only Wald bounds. | principle 21 (the earlier decision) | One rule for totals across the two models. | done (other bound types a gap) |
| The coefficient table is `coefficients(alpha_ci)`, indexed by `(event, covariate)`, with the columns of the regressions' coefficient table (`coef`, `exp(coef)`, `se(coef)`, the two intervals, `z`, `p`); `exp(coef)` is the rate ratio. `summary(alpha_ci)` keeps the shape of `CauseSpecificNHPP.summary` (every parameter, baseline first, indexed by `(event, parameter)`). | proposed | `summary` means "every fitted parameter" on both pieces, so the table of effects needed its own name; its columns are the ones a user of `CoxPH.summary` already reads. The alternative is `summary()` being the coefficient table, as on the Cox models. | proposed |
| `to_dict` writes each type's model and its parameter covariance (`"covariances"`, optional on reading), as `CauseSpecificNHPP` does; a restored model gives the same bounds, `coefficients()` and `summary()`, and refuses the likelihood and the criteria. `is_tvc` is not saved (as on the single-type model). Schema unchanged. | principle 20 | A saved model keeps its bounds; no new schema is needed for a new model tag. | done |
| Information criteria are the whole model's: the summed log-likelihood, the parameters estimated over every type (an aliased coefficient is not one), and BIC's `n` the events of every type. | principle 21 (`CauseSpecificNHPP`) | The same rule as the model without covariates; with a zero covariate column the two models' criteria are equal (tested). | done |
| One aliasing warning for the fits of all types (`collect_aliased`, as `CompetingRisksProportionalHazards`); `aliased` is the union over the types. | principle 22 | One warning per problem. | done |
| Not carried over, each a method that raises `NotImplementedError` saying what to use instead: `diagnose`, `residuals`, `cramer_von_mises`, `trend_test`, `plot`, the four simulation methods and `mcf` (each type's own model has them: `models[event]`), `forecast` (and `surpyval.forecast`, which calls the model's refusal), `growth`, `mtbf`, `mtbf_cb`, `total_mtbf`, `total_mtbf_cb`. | principle 2, 22 | The types are Poisson processes of their own, so the per-type calls are valid today; a combined diagnosis, a superposed simulation with marks and a forecast with covariates (per item or along a schedule) each need a design of their own. A refusal that names the working call is better than an `AttributeError`. | gap |

### Several failure modes of a repairable system: bounds, growth, forecasts (11 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| The names on `CauseSpecificNHPP` are the single-process ones with the cause as `event` (`cif_cb`, `iif_cb`, `mtbf`, `mtbf_cb`, each the cause's own model's), and `total_` in front for the sum over causes (`total_iif`, `total_mtbf`, `total_cif_cb`, `total_iif_cb`, `total_mtbf_cb`), following the existing `total_cif`. The totals have Wald bounds only: Crow's exact bounds are for one power-law process. | principle 21 | One vocabulary; `total_cif` was already there. | done |
| A total's variance is the sum of the causes' delta-method variances on the natural scale, and its bounds are then formed on the log scale, as each cause's are. | proposed | The causes of a marked Poisson process are fitted to disjoint events, so their estimates are independent; the same scale as the single-process bounds makes a one-cause model's total bounds that cause's (tested), and for constant rates gives exactly the bounds of one HPP fitted to every event (tested by hand). Coverage studied: 0.943 to 0.961 at 95% (seed 403, 1000 replicates). | done |
| `growth()` reads the trend from one parameter of the baseline (`beta` of Crow-AMSAA and Cox-Lewis, `alpha` of Duane) and tests it against its no-trend value (1, 0, 1) with a Wald test on the scale of `param_cb`'s interval (the log of a positive parameter), so `trend` is `"none"` exactly when the interval contains that value. The words are the trend tests' (`"increasing"`, `"decreasing"`, `"none"`), about the intensity; a parameter with no variance gives `"undetermined"`. | proposed | The interval and the test cannot disagree; a likelihood-ratio test would need a constrained refit the intensity fitters do not offer. The share of the total intensity has no bounds yet (a gap). | done |
| `growth()` refuses a model fitted with `dist=HPP`, naming `dist` and the baselines to refit with. | principle 2 | A constant rate has no trend parameter; reporting "none" would present an assumption as a finding. | done |
| Forecasts mirror `surpyval.forecast`: `CauseSpecificNHPP.forecast(age, horizon, n, limit, alpha_ci, event)` returns its `Forecast` for one type or, without `event`, for every type, and `surpyval.forecast(model, ...)` is the latter. The interval is the Poisson one at the fitted intensities; it does not carry parameter uncertainty, as the single-process forecast does not. | proposed | The sum of independent Poisson counts is Poisson, so the total needs nothing new; an interval that carried the fit's uncertainty should be added to every Poisson-process forecast at once, not to this model alone (a gap, said in the docs). No table of every cause in one call: the total's row would need a label that could collide with a cause's. | done |
| `CauseSpecificNHPP.to_dict` now writes each cause's parameter covariance (`"covariances"`, `null` for a fit without a likelihood) and the end of observation (`"end"`); the restored model computes its Wald bounds, `growth()` and `summary()` from them. Both keys are optional on reading, so the schema is unchanged and an older dict loads as before (its bounds refuse with the restored model's message). The single-process `ParametricRecurrenceModel` dict is unchanged, so a restored single-process model still refuses its bounds. | proposed | A saved multi-mode model is of little use without its bounds, and the covariance is all a Wald bound needs. Leaving the single-process format alone keeps this change to one model; saving its covariance too would make the two consistent and is the natural next step (open). | done |
| `LikelihoodInferenceMixin.covariance()` is split into `_covariance_matrix()` (the matrix and whether it was found, no warning) and the public method, which warns as before. | proposed | `to_dict` and the tables report a missing covariance as `null` / `nan` without a warning, and package code must not silence warnings with `catch_warnings`. | done |
### Time-varying covariates in the proportional-intensity models (11 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| The fitters take the life regressions' names and shapes unchanged: `fit_tvc(i, xl, xr, c, Z, n=None, ...)`, `fit_tvc_timeline(i, x, Z, c, n=None, ...)`, `fit_tvc_from_df` and `fit_tvc_timeline_from_df`, and the model `cif_tvc(x, Z, xl=None, given=None)`, `iif_tvc(x, Z, xl=None)` and `cb_tvc(x, Z, xl=None, given=None, on="cif", alpha_ci, bound)`, with `Z` a `StepSchedule` or rows with `xl`. | principle 21 | One vocabulary for time-varying covariates; a user who knows `sf_tvc` / `cb_tvc` knows these. | done |
| A repairable item fails many times, so `c` is read on every row: in start-stop rows any row may end in a failure, and in a timeline each row says whether it is a failure (`c = 0`) or not (`c = 1`); an item's first timeline row is its entry and must be `c = 1` (refused otherwise). The life models read a timeline's `c` on the last row only. | proposed | The one place the recurrent shape must differ; refusing a failure on the entry row is clearer than ignoring it (principle 2). | done |
| `n` keeps the meaning it has in the same models' `fit`: the number of failures on a counted row (`c = 2`, somewhere in `(xl, xr]`), and 1 elsewhere (`n > 1` on a `c = 0` or `c = 1` row is refused). It is not the life models' count weight of a row. | proposed | Consistency within the model (the fixed fit refuses `n > 1` on exact rows) won over consistency with the life `fit_tvc`; failures counted per inspection period with a covariate per period is a common shape of such data. The alternative is `n` as a number of identical items. | proposed |
| Supported: delayed entry (a first `xl > 0`), gaps between an item's rows (not at risk), failure-truncated items, counted rows (`c = 2`). Refused, saying what to give instead: `c = -1` (a `c = 2` row over its interval), a missing covariate or time (the row is part of an item's history: principle 3), overlapping rows. Right truncation (`tr`) has no argument. | proposed | Every start-stop row states its own window, so a left-censored count is a `c = 2` row; the right-truncated likelihood (#924) along a covariate path is a separate piece. | done (`tr` a gap) |
| The time-varying fit has its own likelihood (`recurrent/regression/tvc.py`), and shares the search, the verification of the maximum, aliasing and the inference with `fit`: each fitter's search moved, unchanged, into `_maximise` (fits of the existing fixtures and the Rossi data are bit-identical before and after). | principle 12, 13 | One search to keep right; `maximum` is set the same way. | done |
| BIC and AIC_c of a time-varying fit count failures (exact rows and counts), as every recurrent fit; rows are not units, so cutting a row leaves the criteria unchanged. | principle 5 (in spirit), the life `fit_tvc` rule | The likelihood is unchanged by a cut, so the criteria must be. | done |
| `cif_tvc` counts from the time origin and refuses a schedule that starts later, as `sf_tvc`; with `given` it counts from that age (0 at and before it) and the schedule need only start by then. `iif_tvc` at a step is the intensity just after it (a `StepSchedule` segment is `[a, b)`), while a fitted row `(xl, xr]` gives a failure at `xr` the row's covariates: a difference on a set of times of measure zero. `cb_tvc` bounds `on="cif"` or `"iif"` on the log scale by the delta method, as `cif_cb` and `iif_cb`; `given` with `on="iif"` is refused. | proposed | Mirrors #930 and #962 for the life models; "expected failures from here on" is the question asked of a repairable item in service. | done |
| A model fitted by `fit_tvc` keeps no `data` (it has no one covariate row per item) and has `is_tvc`; `residuals`, `cramer_von_mises`, `trend_test`, `diagnose` and `plot` refuse for it with a `ValueError` naming `fit_tvc` and what to use instead. `cif`, `iif`, `cif_cb`, simulation and `mcf` at one fixed covariate vector work as for any model; given a `StepSchedule` the simulation methods and `mcf` refuse, naming `cif_tvc`. A `CovariatePath` is refused by the three path methods (`NotImplementedError`, as the life models' refusals of a family with no path form). `is_tvc` is not saved in `to_dict`: a restored model has no data whichever way it was fitted. | proposed | A refusal is better than a fixed-covariate answer for covariates that changed (principles 2 and 22); each is a documented gap. | gap |
| The DataFrame forms take `Z_cols` only, no `formula`. | proposed | As this model's `fit_from_df`; its predictions do not encode a formula's terms. | gap |
| Found, not changed: Duane's standard errors and bounds move slightly with the time unit (se of `alpha` 0.37272 in months, 0.37159 in hours, on the test's rows), for the fixed fit too: the numerical Hessian behind `covariance()` at a scale `b` of 3e-4. The estimates and the likelihood are unit-free, and Crow-AMSAA's bounds are. Pinned as a strict xfail in `tests/recurrent/test_proportional_intensity_tvc.py`. | proposed | Outside this change (the shared `numerical_hessian`); reported for the maintainer. | open |
### Warnings held back in one thread only (11 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| The package holds a warning back with `ignoring_warnings()` (`surpyval/utils/warnings.py`), not with `warnings.catch_warnings()` and `simplefilter("ignore")`. The 117 such blocks are converted one for one (same place, same category, same message pattern); `test_thread_safe_warnings.py` fails if a module uses `catch_warnings` again outside the listed places. | proposed | `catch_warnings` saves and restores the one filter list every thread shares: while a block ran, every other thread's warnings were dropped, and two threads leaving blocks out of order left the "ignore" in place for good. In its own thread the new block is the old one (the newest filter wins); the refactor snapshot is bit-exact before and after, the warnings of every registry case included. | done |
| `ignoring_warnings` is a filter in the shared list whose message slot is a `threading.local`: its `match` is looked up in the calling thread, and is "never" except in a thread inside a block. A filter is added at the front of the list when none is there, and never removed; a block only switches its own thread's `match`. Everything the interpreter calls while it reads the list is C (`threading.local` attribute lookup, `functools.partial` of `operator.is_`, a compiled pattern's `match`). | proposed | Python 3.11 to 3.13 have no per-thread filters. A filter that runs Python code while the list is read (`__subclasscheck__`, a Python `match`) lets another thread run half way through the read: a filter removed then makes the reader skip the next one (seen: an "error" filter skipped once in three fits), and a list replaced then can be freed under the reader. With C only, the interpreter lock is held for the whole read. On Python 3.14 run with context-aware warnings the block is the standard form, which is then local to the thread already. | done |
| Six places still use `catch_warnings`: five collect an inner step's warnings to read or repeat them (`fit_best`, the renewal fit's no-maximum check, the Cox frailty fit's Cox fit, the residual-life integral, `diagnose()`'s Cramer-von Mises test), and `formula_model_matrix` catches `formulaic`'s unknown-level warning for a formula naming its levels. | proposed | Collecting warnings needs the shared `showwarning` hook; each needs its own rewrite (reading the step's result instead of its warnings, as the accelerated life starts were), which can change which warnings a user sees. Left as they are, listed in the test, for a later change. | superseded (12 Nov 2026) |

## 2026-11-07

### Thread-safe warning checks (7 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| Library code does not change the process-wide warning filters to detect a warning. The accelerated life fit's per-level start checks the level's fit directly (`np.errstate` with numpy's "warn" settings made "raise", `quiet_maximum_warnings()`, `model.maximum` in verified/not applicable, finite parameters, Kish effective sample size at least 20 with weights) instead of `filterwarnings("error")`. The weights check keeps a fallback whose only cause was the robust-variance warning, which says nothing about the point estimate; it is kept so that every start is the same as before (the old and new decisions agreed on all 4,521 levels fitted by the accelerated life tests, scenarios and conformance cases, and no warning escaped the new path). | proposed | A filter set in one thread applies to all threads, so a fit made other threads' warnings raise. Dropping the weights fallback would change starts (and possibly the last digits of fits) for weighted data; left for the maintainer. | done |
| `formula_model_matrix` finds unknown categorical levels by reading the data for a fitted spec whose categorical terms each code a column as is, and for a formula without `levels=` (whose levels all come from the data); only a formula naming its levels or a term computed from the data (`C(k + 1)`) still catches `formulaic`'s `DataMismatchWarning` with `catch_warnings`. | proposed | Those terms' values exist only inside `formulaic`, and Python 3.11 has no per-thread warning filters; the remaining case is narrow and only affects `DataMismatchWarning`. | done |

## 2026-11-06

### Competing-risks diagnose(): delayed entry and joint fits (6 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| With delayed entry, `diagnose()`'s incidence check on a `ParametricCompetingRisks` model compares with `CompetingRisks.fit(..., tl=)`. When every unit enters after 0 that estimate is conditional on surviving to the earliest entry `s`, so the model's incidence is conditioned the same way, `(CIF_k(t) - CIF_k(s)) / S(s)` with `S` the all-cause survival, and the gap is taken from `s` on. The bootstrap draws each cause's latent time beyond the unit's entry (`qf(F_k(tl) + U (1 - F_k(tl)))`), keeps the earliest, censors as before (the censoring distribution already counts units at risk from entry) and refits every cause with the same `tl`. Data without entry times take exactly the old path (checked bit for bit against dev: draws, gaps and p-values). Right truncation (only possible through `from_fitted`) still leaves the incidence rows uncomputed, saying why. | proposed | The Aalen-Johansen estimate takes `tl` since #1105; the conditioning is what that estimate estimates (as `KaplanMeier` with `tl`), and drawing beyond entry is how the data were sampled. | done |
| Size with delayed entry (`tests/calibration/test_size_parametric_competing_risks_diagnostics.py`, seed 291, 200 replicates, n = 100, each unit entering at U(0, 5) and kept only if still in service then, 49 refits, judged at 10%, MC se 0.021): AD rejected 0.070 (a) and 0.030 (b); incidence 0.110 (a) and 0.045 (b). All within the study's tolerance (0.074). Cause b's incidence and AD are conservative here (b's AD is already the univariate test's conservative case with censoring). | proposed | Seed, size and targets fixed before the run; reported as found. | done |
| `diagnose()` on a `ParametricCompetingRisks` fitted to left- or interval-censored rows (a joint fit) refuses with a `NotImplementedError` of its own: the causes are fitted together and keep no data, and every check needs each failure's exact time; it says to compare distributions by `aic()` and the all-cause `sf` with `Turnbull.fit` of the same windows. No check is run on the joint fit: Cox-Snell residuals for interval-censored rows and an Aalen-Johansen estimate for windows are not straightforward, and the joint fit holds no data to run them on. A model restored with `from_dict` (joint or not) keeps the restored model's message; the distinction is an unsaved `_fitted_jointly` flag set by the joint fit. | proposed | An accurate refusal is better than a check that is not valid; the restored message was wrong for a fitted joint model. | done |
| A joint fit's dict (with its `"joint"` entry: the covariance between causes, `neg_ll`, `k`, `n`) is stamped schema 4, so SurPyval 0.26 and older refuse it with a request to upgrade rather than load it without the terms between causes. Every other `ParametricCompetingRisks` dict is unchanged (schema 1 or 2 as before, loadable by old readers). Supersedes the 5d row "`to_dict` of a joint fit adds an optional `"joint"` entry ... no schema change". | maintainer (2026-11-06) | The same pattern as weighted models (B6): a refusal is better than an older reader silently using a smaller covariance. | done |

## 2026-11-05

### 0.27 WP2b: uncertainty of a fixed probability (5 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| A fitted `Bernoulli`, `FixedEventProbability` or `Binomial` has `covariance()` = `p_hat (1 - p_hat) / N` from its event counts (the inverse observed information), and `standard_errors()`. The Binomial's is 2 x 2 over `[n, p]` with a zero row and column for the known `n`, as a held parameter has, rather than 1 x 1. Names come from the base class's `covariance_names` (no per-model attribute). | proposed | Consistency: `covariance()` follows `parameter_names` on every `Parametric`. | done |
| At `k = 0` or `k = N` the variance is `nan`, not 0, and is not a refusal: `covariance()` returns `[[nan]]`, `standard_errors()` `nan`; the docs point to `param_cb("p")`. | proposed | 0 would tell propagation code `p` is known after, say, no failures in 20 demands; the package's Wald bound is already `nan` there, and `standard_errors_of` documents `nan` for a parameter on a boundary. A refusal would need its own message in the base class, which this change does not touch. | done |
| No new `p_cb`: the Jeffreys interval is `param_cb("p", method="jeffreys")`, beside `"exact"`, `"wald"` and `"lr"`. The default stays `"exact"` (Clopper-Pearson). | proposed | `param_cb` already routes these models to bounds from the counts; one entry point. Exact holds its level at every `p` (study: 0.968 to 0.995 at 95%); Jeffreys fell to 0.883 and 0.904 in two cells, and changing the default would move published numbers. | done |
| Jeffreys at `k = 0` / `k = N`: lower 0 / upper 1, with the other bound still at `alpha / 2` (two-sided), as Clopper-Pearson does. R's `binom::binom.confint(methods = "bayes")` moves the whole `alpha` to the open tail there. | proposed | Same convention as the exact interval in the same function; Brown, Cai and DasGupta (2001). | done |
| No `p_random` / posterior draw method on the fitted model. Draws of `p` come from `Bernoulli.fit_bayes(x, priors={"p": Beta.from_params([0.5, 0.5])})`, the conjugate Jeffreys posterior. | proposed | The Bayesian module already gives it (and `draw_models`); `Parametric` has no parameter-draw convention to follow. | done |
| These fits take no `w` (they already raise `WeightedRefusal`); counts go in `n`, so no robust variance is needed. | proposed | No silent wrong variance. | done |

### 0.27 WP2e: GeneralLinear life model (5 Nov 2026)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| `GeneralLinear` is `L(Z) = a + b'Z`: a positive coefficient means a later (longer) life, the sign of every life model (`Linear`'s `a + bV`, `GeneralLogLinear`'s `c e^{b'Z}`), not the AFT/PH sign (positive = shorter). Its signs are then R's `survreg(..., dist = "gaussian")` signs, with no flip. | proposed | Consistency with the life models it extends: one column of `GeneralLinear` is `Linear` exactly. The one-sign-convention rule (2026-11-03) is defined by the existing models, and accelerated life models write the life itself. The alternative, `a - b'Z`, would make `GeneralLinear` the only life model with a minus sign. | proposed |
| Parameters mirror `GeneralLogLinear`: the constant `a` (unbounded, as `Linear`'s) then `coef_0`, `coef_1`, ... (or the column names), stored unresolved and rebuilt from `n_stresses`; start from least squares through the per-level lives. It is not added to the accelerated-life diagnostics' candidate list, which compares life models of a time scale. | proposed | Same shape, same serialisation, nothing new to learn. | proposed |

### Weighted model dicts: each matrix under its own name

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| A weighted model's dict stores every matrix under its true name: `"hess_inv"` is always the inverse Hessian (as the attribute is) and the robust covariance is `"covariance"`, the key every model's dict already stores `covariance()` under, so no new key. The `"inverse_hessian"` key is gone. Every weighted model (`"weighted": true`) is stamped schema 4, which 0.26 (reading up to schema 3) refuses with a request to upgrade rather than reading the inverse Hessian as the robust covariance; weighted data alone stays schema 3. This supersedes the 2026-11-03 row "A weighted model's dict writes the inverse Hessian as `"inverse_hessian"` ..." and, for models, the schema 3 of the weights rows. | maintainer (2026-11-05) | One meaning per key, as per attribute (principle 21); a refusal is better than a 0.26 reader silently using the smaller matrix. Only the parametric distributions write `"hess_inv"`; the other weighted fitters (regressions, Cox, Aalen, Turnbull, competing risks) already wrote only `"covariance"`, and change only their schema. | done |
| The readers: a weighted parametric dict stamped schema 4 reads `"hess_inv"` as the inverse Hessian. One stamped 3 or less with no `"inverse_hessian"` (written by 0.26) loads as before: no `hess_inv` (its `"hess_inv"` was the robust covariance; the inverse Hessian cannot be rebuilt without the data), bounds from `"covariance"`. One with `"inverse_hessian"` (written by the development versions between 2026-11-03 and this change) takes `hess_inv` from that key. | maintainer (2026-11-05) | Every weighted file written so far keeps its bounds. | done |

### Restricted mean survival time of the parametric models

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Parametric.rmst(tau, alpha_ci)` returns the non-parametric `rmst`'s dict (`rmst`, `se`, `lower`, `upper`, `tau`); `se` is the delta method's on the RMST itself, and the interval is the Wald interval on `logit(rmst / tau)`, which `rmst_cb(method="wald")` also gives. | proposed | The same call and result as the non-parametric models; the logit keeps the bounds in (0, tau), where a plain or log interval can pass tau when the RMST is near it. Matches R's delta method on the same scale to 1e-6. | done |
| `rmst()` refuses a model with no covariance (built with `from_params`, not fitted by MLE), naming `mean(tau=tau)`, which gives the point alone, as the non-parametric `mean(tau)` does. | proposed | A dict with `nan` bounds would hide why; the non-parametric `rmst` refuses a model without a variance too. | done |
| The RMST is the area from 0 for every distribution, a whole-line one (Normal, Gumbel, Logistic) included: the time lived in [0, tau]. A `tau` past the data is allowed (the fit extrapolates); the default `tau` is the largest observed time, as for the non-parametric models. Discrete distributions are refused (their RMST is a sum of `sf` over the counts). | proposed | One definition across models; the non-parametric refusal past the data is about a curve that is not estimated there, which a parametric fit is. | done |
| The regression models' `rmst_cb` has Wald and bootstrap bounds, not likelihood-ratio ones: `method="lr"` is refused with that message. | proposed | The regression likelihood-ratio search is built per function (sf, quantiles); a search over an integral at every step is a separate piece. | gap |

### Weights stage 2: the Aalen-Johansen cumulative incidence (`CompetingRisks`)

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| `CompetingRisks.fit(..., w=)` and `fit_from_df(..., w_col=)`: each unit counts `n * w` in the numbers at risk and of each cause's failures; every variance is the infinitesimal jackknife, `sum_i n_i w_i^2 U_i(t)^2` (no small-sample factor), as R's `survfit(Surv(x, factor(e)) ~ 1, weights = w)$std.err` (checked to 1e-10, ties included). | principle 16 | The stage-1 rule for weights (robust variance always); R's multi-state `survfit` is the reference. | done |
| With `w = 1` the cumulative incidence's variance is the robust one, not Aalen's, which the fit without `w` keeps. | proposed | One variance per kind of fit, as the weighted Kaplan-Meier and log-rank test. | done |
| The bounds on `sf`, `ff` and `Hf` of a weighted model are those of the weighted `KaplanMeier` / `NelsonAalen` fit of the same rows (all causes, or the cause with the others censored), by the fitted `how`; the stacked plot's all-cause bounds are the weighted Kaplan-Meier's. | proposed | As the unweighted model, which borrows the single-event estimates' bounds. | done |
| A weighted model's dict stores the robust variances (`cif_var`, `log_sf_var`, `log_sf_var_km`) and `effective_n`, and is stamped schema 3. | principle 20 | A reader without weights would bound the estimate with Aalen's variance. | done |
| `gray_test(..., w=)` refuses with a `WeightedRefusal` naming `logrank(..., w=)` and the weighted cumulative incidences' bounds. | proposed | `cmprsk` has no weighted Gray test to check one against. | gap |
| No delayed entry: the estimate takes none, weighted or not. | proposed | Out of this change's scope. | gap |

### Firth's penalised Cox fit: `CoxPH.fit_firth`

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
| A separate method, `CoxPH.fit_firth(x, Z, c, n, tl, strata, alpha_ci, profile, center, tol)`, in `cox_firth.py`, returning the usual Cox model with a `firth` result (`FirthResult`, in `to_dict`). | proposed | As `fit_penalised`: a different estimator with its own inference; the same model type predicts and serialises as any Cox model. | done |
| Breslow ties only, no `tie_method`. | proposed | R's `coxphf`, the reference, has only Breslow's; the penalty's derivatives are written for it. | done |
| The covariance is the inverse of the unpenalised information at the Firth estimate, `I(beta_hat)^-1`, not of the penalised curvature. | proposed | What `coxphf` reports (checked to 1e-10) and Heinze and Schemper use. | done |
| Intervals and p-values by the profile penalised likelihood by default (`coxphf`'s `pl = TRUE`), Wald with `profile=False`; `model.p_values` and `summary()` show the chosen ones, at the fitted `alpha_ci` (another level refuses, naming the refit). `z` stays Wald's. | proposed | Under separation the profile is flat on one side and the Wald interval is far too narrow there. | done |
| `likelihood_ratio_test()` of a Firth fit is the penalised test against all coefficients 0 (`coxphf`'s); `neg_ll()`, AIC and BIC stay those of the unpenalised partial likelihood at the estimate. | proposed | The penalised statistic is the one that goes with the estimate; the criteria keep one meaning across Cox fits. | superseded (2026-11-05): the next row |
| A Firth fit refuses `aic()`, `bic()` and `aic_c()` with a `FirthRefusal` (a `ValueError`) saying why and naming the ordinary refit (`CoxPH.fit`) and the penalised `likelihood_ratio_test()`; the printout says so in one line. `likelihood_ratio_test()` stays the penalised test (`coxphf`'s) and `neg_ll()` the unpenalised partial likelihood at the estimate. | maintainer (2026-11-05) | The penalised likelihood is not a likelihood to compare models by, and the ordinary one is not at its maximum at the penalised estimate, so a criterion from either misleads; refused as a weighted fit's criteria are. | done |
| A column the partial likelihood cannot determine is refused, not aliased. | proposed | The penalty, a log determinant, is not defined where the information is singular. | done |
| The monotone-likelihood warning of a Cox fit names `CoxPH.fit_firth` as the fix; the Fine-Gray and weighted Cox fits, which it does not cover, keep the general advice. | proposed | The warning should name a fix that the caller's fit can use. | done |
| No weights `w` yet. | proposed | `coxphf` has none to check against. | open |

### Brier score and time-dependent AUC for competing risks

| Decision | By | Reason | Status |
|---|---|---|---|
